<a id="eq:contact-count"></a>
For integers $0\le a<b$, denote by $\mathcal C(a,b]$ the event of a contact in at least one block with index $a<j\le b$. The quantity to be estimated is $$\label{eq:contact-count}
 m(J)=\sum_{j=1}^J\mathbf P\bigl(\mathcal C(2^j,2^{j+1}]\bigr),
 \qquad J\ge1.$$ It is the expected number of dyadic block-index intervals containing a contact. Section <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">5</a> bounds it above using the finite first moment of the lateral increment $S$; the remaining sections obtain an incompatible lower bound from the forced contact near the top and the renewal-tail estimate.

<a id="sec:entropy"></a>
### An entropy upper bound for contacts

We continue under the assumption that both coordinate signs have positive probability. The opposed arrangement and its common blocks are those of Section <a href="#sec:arrangement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arrangement">4</a>. We will prove

<a id="stmt-prop-entropy-upper"></a>
<div id="prop:entropy-upper" class="proposition">

**Proposition 14**. *Suppose that both coordinate signs have positive directional-transience probability. In the opposed arrangement of Section <a href="#sec:arrangement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arrangement">4</a>, let $m(J)$ be the expected number of dyadic common-block index intervals $(2^j,2^{j+1}]$, $1\le j\le J$, containing a departure-site contact, as in <a href="#eq:contact-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-count">[eq:contact-count]</a>. Then $$m(J)=O\!\left(\frac{J}{\log J}\right)\qquad(J\longrightarrow\infty).$$*

</div>

The argument compares a piece of the opposed arrangement with independent bridges of prescribed heights. Contacts are rare under the bridge law. The cost of this comparison is controlled by the entropy gained when more independent common-block displacements are added.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A randomized comparison with independent bridges

Fix an integer $n\ge1$. We want to cover the common blocks with indices in $(4n,8n]$ by a middle portion of the arrangement. We will surround it by two outer portions, each of height at least $n$, so that any middle contact occurs well inside a finite slab. A buffer above all three portions supplies an independent lateral gap.

<a id="eq:entropy-count-windows"></a>
We randomize the number of blocks in each portion as well as in the buffer. Choosing a chunk count uniformly from $n$ possibilities will offset the cost of specifying its random height. Randomizing the buffer count will let us compare its entropy with that of the whole experiment without paying an additional $\log n$. Independently of the common-block tape, choose four independent integer counts $$\label{eq:entropy-count-windows}
 \begin{split}
 U,I_1,I_3&\ \hbox{uniform on }\{n,\ldots,2n-1\},\\
 I_2&\ \hbox{uniform on }\{7n,\ldots,8n-1\}.
 \end{split}$$ Read a buffer of $U$ common blocks, followed by three chunks of $I_1,I_2,I_3$ common blocks. Let $d_0$ be the lateral gap after the buffer, namely the sum of its gap increments. For chunk $i$, let $A_i$ and $B_i$ be the positive and negative slab lists in their downward tape order, and let $H_i$ be their common total width. Write $$H=(H_1,H_2,H_3),\qquad
 \mathcal A=(A_1,A_2,A_3),\qquad
 \mathcal B=(B_1,B_2,B_3).$$ The three chunks are independent, and $(U,d_0)$ is independent of all their data. To see this despite their random boundaries, condition on the four external counts and use the product law of the common blocks; the distribution of a chunk depends only on its own count. The first and third count windows ensure the required outer heights. The longer middle window ensures that the middle chunk starts before block $4n$ and ends after block $8n$.

Set $q=d-1$, and let $X_{A,i},X_{B,i}\in\mathbb Z^q$ be the sums of the lateral displacements of the words in $A_i,B_i$, measured in the words’ actual chronological directions. The total positive displacement determines where its bottom lies relative to its top. Accordingly, define <a id="eq:entropy-alignment-variables"></a>$$\label{eq:entropy-alignment-variables}
 X_A=\sum_{i=1}^3X_{A,i},\quad X_B=\sum_{i=1}^3X_{B,i},\quad
 Z=d_0+X_A,\quad T=U+I_1+I_2+I_3.$$ Here is the placement that explains the alignment variable $Z$ and will define the same contact event under the actual and reference laws. Set $N_*=H_1+H_2+H_3$. Place the positive chunks from height $0$ and lateral position $0$, in chronological order $3,2,1$, reversing the list order within each chunk. Each word is still traversed in its original direction. Place the negative chunks in chronological order $1,2,3$ from height $N_*-1$ and lateral position $Z$. Let $E_{\mathrm{mid}}$ be a departure contact between the two middle chunks.

For these actual chunk data, this placement reproduces the infinite opposed arrangement after its buffer, up to translation. For example, at the top of the middle chunk the lateral gap, negative minus positive, is $$Z+X_{B,1}-(X_{A,3}+X_{A,2})
   =d_0+X_{A,1}+X_{B,1},$$ as in the original arrangement; the subsequent list placements also agree. From <a href="#eq:entropy-count-windows" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:entropy-count-windows">[eq:entropy-count-windows]</a>, the middle chunk starts after at most $4n-2$ common blocks and ends after at least $9n$ blocks. It therefore contains the deterministic common-block interval $(4n,8n]$.

Denote the actual law of $(H,Z,\mathcal A,\mathcal B)$ by $P_n$. Define a reference law $Q_n$ as follows. First sample $(H,Z)$ with its actual joint marginal. Given these values, sample the six lists independently, with respective exact-cut bridge laws $\mathcal B_{H_i}^+$ and $\mathcal B_{H_i}^-$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-bridge">11</a>. The reference retains the dependence between $H$ and $Z$; it changes only the conditional law of the lists. Apply the same placement rule to its newly sampled lists. Thus $E_{\mathrm{mid}}$ is one fixed measurable event in both experiments, and its actual probability bounds the original contact probability in $(4n,8n]$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The entropy cost of the comparison

We use entropy and relative entropy in their classical information-theoretic sense (Shannon 1948; Kullback and Leibler 1951) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Shannon1948](#ref-Shannon1948), [KullbackLeibler1951](#ref-KullbackLeibler1951)], recalling the identities and bounds needed for the countable path spaces below.

For a countable random variable $V$, write $$\mathcal H(V)=-\sum_v P(V=v)\log P(V=v),$$ with $0\log0=0$. Conditional entropy is the average of the corresponding conditional entropies. For probability measures $P,Q$ on a countable set, their relative entropy is $$D(P\Vert Q)=\sum_x P(x)\log\frac{P(x)}{Q(x)},$$ with value $+\infty$ if $P$ is not absolutely continuous with respect to $Q$. Conditional mutual information $I(V;W\mid H)$ is the average, over $H$, of the relative entropy between the joint conditional law and the product of its two marginals.

We use the chain rule for relative entropy, obtained by factoring joint probabilities into marginal and conditional probabilities. In particular, if a reference law for $(V,W)$ conditional on $H$ factors between $V$ and $W$, the averaged conditional relative entropy is at least $I(V;W\mid H)$. Nonnegativity of relative entropy follows from Jensen’s inequality applied to $-\log$. The same factorization, followed by nonnegativity, shows that applying a measurable map cannot increase relative entropy. These facts apply to countable path lists without assuming that the lists themselves have finite entropy; equivalently one may first use finite partitions and pass to their increasing limit.

<a id="eq:gap-entropy-growth"></a>
Let $S_1,S_2,\ldots$ be the iid lateral gap increments of the common blocks, and set $$h_k=\mathcal H(S_1+\cdots+S_k),\qquad k\ge1.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:common-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-blocks">13</a> gives $\mathbf E|S_1|<\infty$. For a $\mathbb Z^q$-valued variable with finite first moment, comparison with the probability weights proportional to $\exp(-|x|/(k+1))$ gives $$\label{eq:gap-entropy-growth}
 h_k\le C+q\log(k+1).$$ Indeed the normalizing sum is at most $C_q(k+1)^q$, and the expected comparison energy is at most $k\mathbf E|S_1|/(k+1)$. Also $h_k$ is nondecreasing: condition on the last independent summand, use translation invariance of entropy, and then remove the conditioning.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:common-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-blocks">13</a>, the sum of the slab radii in a common block has finite expectation. Thus each tape’s block displacement, and not only their sum $S$, has a finite first absolute moment. In addition the common width has finite mean. All count, height, and displacement variables used below therefore have first moments of order $n$, and their joint and conditional entropies are finite. No entropy bound for the full path lists is needed.

<a id="stmt-lem-entropy-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:entropy-comparison" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15**. *There is a constant $C$, independent of $n$, such that $$D(P_n\Vert Q_n)\le C+h_{14n}-h_n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-entropy-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-entropy-comparison</summary>

<a id="eq:count-window-density"></a>
*Proof.* There are two costs to control: imposing the random chunk heights, and then retaining the aligned starting displacement $Z$. We first condition on $H$ alone. For a specified pair of slab lists of common total width $h$, let $r$ be the number of their common positive cumulative widths, including $h$. The pair is uniquely parsed into its first $r$ common blocks. If $r$ belongs to the count window for chunk $i$, its actual unconditioned mass is $$\frac1n\prod_{a\in A_i}W(a)\prod_{b\in B_i}W(b).$$ It has zero mass otherwise. The independent exact-cut bridge pair at height $h$ has mass given by the same product divided by $u_h^+u_h^-$. Thus the conditional density of the actual pair, on its support, is $$\label{eq:count-window-density}
 \frac{u_h^+u_h^-}{nP_n(H_i=h)}.$$ Common cuts here are counted in the original downward list order, before any list is reversed for chronological placement.

<a id="eq:conditional-tape-information"></a>
Let $D_0$ be the averaged conditional relative entropy of $(\mathcal A,\mathcal B)$ given $H$ with respect to these independent bridges. The chunks are independent, so <a href="#eq:count-window-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:count-window-density">[eq:count-window-density]</a> and $u_h^\pm\le1$ give $$D_0\le\sum_{i=1}^3\bigl(\mathcal H(H_i)-\log n\bigr)\le C.$$ For the last bound, $\mathbf E H_i\le8n\mathbf E K$, and a positive integer variable with mean $a$ has entropy at most $1+\log(a+1)$, by comparison with a geometric distribution. Since the reference conditional on $H$ factors between the tapes, $$\label{eq:conditional-tape-information}
 I(\mathcal A;\mathcal B\mid H)\le D_0.$$

<a id="eq:endpoint-information-cost"></a>
Introducing $Z$ while retaining its actual marginal costs precisely its conditional mutual information with the lists. The chain rule and the independence of the buffer give $$\label{eq:endpoint-information-cost}
 \begin{split}
 D(P_n\Vert Q_n)
 &=D_0+I(Z;\mathcal A,\mathcal B\mid H)\\
 &=D_0+\mathcal H(Z\mid H)-\mathcal H(d_0).
 \end{split}$$ Conditional on $\mathcal A,H$, the variable $Z$ is obtained by adding the independent $d_0$. Data processing in <a href="#eq:conditional-tape-information" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:conditional-tape-information">[eq:conditional-tape-information]</a> therefore yields $I(Z;\mathcal B\mid H)\le D_0$.

The direct bound $\mathcal H(Z\mid H)=O(\log n)$ would be too large after summing over scales. Instead, add the negative tape’s displacement to $Z$. Their sum is the total common-block gap, whose entropy can be compared with that of the buffer. Conditioning on the negative tape costs at most $D_0$, as just proved:

<a id="eq:buffer-entropy-chain"></a>
$$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal H(T,Z+X_B)
 &\ge \mathcal H(T,Z+X_B\mid\mathcal B,H)
  =\mathcal H(T,Z\mid\mathcal B,H)\notag\\
 &\ge \mathcal H(Z\mid H)-D_0
       +\mathcal H(T\mid Z,\mathcal A,\mathcal B,H)\notag\\
 &=\mathcal H(Z\mid H)-D_0+\mathcal H(U\mid d_0).
 \label{eq:buffer-entropy-chain}
\end{aligned}$$ For the last equality, the two lists in each chunk determine its common count. Knowing these lists and $Z$ therefore fixes both $T-U$ and $d_0=Z-X_A$. The buffer pair is independent of the chunks.

<a id="eq:random-count-entropy-difference"></a>
The vector $Z+X_B$ is the sum of the first $T$ common gap increments. The count $T$, like $U$, is independent of the underlying iid tape; we are not conditioning on the observed chunks in the following identities. Consequently $$\mathcal H(T,Z+X_B)=\mathcal H(T)+\mathbf E h_T,
 \qquad
 \mathcal H(U,d_0)=\log n+\mathbf E h_U.$$ Here $n\le U<2n$ and $10n\le T\le14n-4$, so $T$ takes at most $4n$ values. Monotonicity of $h_k$ gives $$\label{eq:random-count-entropy-difference}
 \mathcal H(T,Z+X_B)-\mathcal H(U,d_0)
 \le\log4+h_{14n}-h_n.$$ Subtract $\mathcal H(d_0)$ in <a href="#eq:buffer-entropy-chain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:buffer-entropy-chain">[eq:buffer-entropy-chain]</a> and use $\mathcal H(U,d_0)=\mathcal H(d_0)+\mathcal H(U\mid d_0)$. Together with <a href="#eq:endpoint-information-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-information-cost">[eq:endpoint-information-cost]</a> and <a href="#eq:random-count-entropy-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:random-count-entropy-difference">[eq:random-count-entropy-difference]</a>, this gives explicitly $$D(P_n\Vert Q_n)\le 2D_0+\log4+h_{14n}-h_n.$$ The bound for $D_0$ is uniform in $n$, so its doubled contribution is absorbed into $C$. This proves Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-comparison">15</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Rare contacts under the reference law

Comparing opposed paths in one environment and classifying intersection sites by quenched escape probabilities already appears in the planar argument of Zerner (Zerner 2007, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Zerner2007](#ref-Zerner2007)]. We prove the finite-barrier estimate needed here.

<a id="stmt-lem-reference-contact"></a>
<div id="lem:reference-contact" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *For the reference experiment just defined, $$Q_n(E_{\mathrm{mid}})
 \le\delta_n:=\min\{1,Cn(F_+(n)+F_-(n))\}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reference-contact"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reference-contact</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $(H,Z)$ in its retained support; in particular $H_1,H_3\ge n$. Write $N=N_*$. By list-reversal invariance in Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-bridge">11</a>, the placed chronological chunks have their corresponding raw bridge laws.

Retain the complete positive lower and middle chunks, concatenated into a path $\alpha$ from height $0$ to its first hit of $N-H_1$. Retain the negative upper and middle chunks only up to their first arrival at a departure site of $\alpha$. A middle contact ensures that this arrival occurs before the negative middle endpoint at height $H_3-1$. The contact site $y$ must therefore satisfy <a id="eq:reference-contact-height"></a>$$\label{eq:reference-contact-height}
 H_3\le y_1\le N-H_1-1.$$ Thus each path has made at least $n$ progress in its own signed height before reaching the contact, and neither has crossed the global barriers $-1,N$.

We explain the comparison with two paths in one environment before using any quenched exit probabilities. The unused positive upper chunk and negative lower chunk integrate to one. The remaining four bridge normalizations are bounded by $p_+^{-2}p_-^{-2}$. Consecutive bridges within a sign have disjoint departure layers, so their raw weights multiply to the raw weight of their concatenation. Summing over the negative completion after its retained prefix, and dropping its remaining bridge requirements, costs at most that prefix’s raw weight: in each fixed environment the total conditional completion probability is at most one, and this inequality can then be averaged. This argument allows the completion to revisit its prefix’s departure rows and does not factor their weights independently. The first-hit chunk boundaries determine the splits, so no extra multiplicity is introduced.

The retained negative prefix has no departure site in $\operatorname{Dep}(\alpha)$: its terminal point is the first arrival there. Repeated sites within either path cause no difficulty. Lemma <a href="#lem:departure-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:departure-transfer">2</a> therefore identifies the product of their raw weights with the weight of the two paths sampled independently conditional on a common iid environment. For each $\alpha$ the first contact fixes the negative prefix uniquely, and $\alpha$ itself is stopped at a fixed first-hit height. We may thus sum these joint prefix weights without overcounting, and extend the paths as raw trajectories in the common environment.

Only now, in that shared environment, define for interior sites $$Q_y^\omega=P_{y,\omega}(T_{-1}<T_N),$$ where the barriers are absolute first-coordinate heights. If $Q_y^\omega\ge1/2$ at the contact, the positive raw path has visited such a site after hitting $n$ and before exiting the global slab. Conditional on the environment, stop it at its first such visit. The probability of a subsequent exit at $-1$ is at least $1/2$. Consequently the annealed probability of this visit event is at most $$2P_0(T_n<T_{-1}<T_N)=2(u_n^+-u_N^+).$$ If $Q_y^\omega<1/2$, the negative marginal gives the analogous bound $2(u_n^--u_N^-)$. Indeed the almost-sure finite-height-interval exit property established in Section <a href="#sec:arrangement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arrangement">4</a> holds quenched for all these starting sites and barriers outside one null set. The probability of the other exit is therefore greater than $1/2$, with no common lower bound on the transition rows. Measured from the negative start $N-1$, the global barriers $N$ and $-1$ have signed relative heights $-1$ and $N$, respectively. Lateral translation by $Z$ does not change its annealed law.

Combining these alternatives with the bounded normalizations gives, uniformly in $(H,Z)$, $$Q_n(E_{\mathrm{mid}}\mid H,Z)
 \le C\sum_{\sigma\in\{+,-\}}(u_n^\sigma-u_N^\sigma)
 \le CN\bigl(F_+(n)+F_-(n)\bigr),$$ where the last step is Lemma <a href="#lem:renewal-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:renewal-tail">12</a>. Finally $\mathbf E N\le12n\mathbf E K$. Average over the retained joint marginal of $(H,Z)$ and take the minimum with $1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Summing over scales

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-reference-contact-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-reference-contact</summary>

<a id="eq:binary-contact-entropy"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:entropy-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:entropy-upper">14</a>.* For $l\ge2$, take $n=2^{l-2}$ and abbreviate $\delta_l=\delta_n$. The event $E_{\mathrm{mid}}$ contains the original contact event in common blocks $(2^l,2^{l+1}]$. If $\delta_l=0$, absolute continuity from Lemma <a href="#lem:entropy-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:entropy-comparison">15</a> makes its actual probability zero. Otherwise the binary relative-entropy inequality gives $$\label{eq:binary-contact-entropy}
 P_n(E_{\mathrm{mid}})\log(1/\delta_l)
 \le C+h_{14n}-h_n.$$ For completeness, applying data processing to an event with actual and reference probabilities $a,b$ gives binary divergence $a\log(a/b)+(1-a)\log((1-a)/(1-b))$. It is at least $a\log(1/b)-\log2$. Use $b\le\delta_l$ and absorb $\log2$ into $C$.

Width integrability gives $$\sum_{l\ge2}\delta_l<\infty.$$ Among $2\le l\le J$, at most $C\sqrt J$ indices have $\delta_l>J^{-1/2}$. They contribute at most $C\sqrt J$ to $m(J)$. For every other index with positive $\delta_l$, the logarithm in <a href="#eq:binary-contact-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binary-contact-entropy">[eq:binary-contact-entropy]</a> is at least $\tfrac12\log J$. Since $14n\le16n$, monotonicity and bounded overlap give $$\sum_{l=2}^J\bigl(h_{14\cdot2^{l-2}}-h_{2^{l-2}}\bigr)
 \le\sum_{l=2}^J\bigl(h_{2^{l+2}}-h_{2^{l-2}}\bigr)
 \le4h_{2^{J+2}}=O(J),$$ using <a href="#eq:gap-entropy-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gap-entropy-growth">[eq:gap-entropy-growth]</a>. Summing <a href="#eq:binary-contact-entropy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:binary-contact-entropy">[eq:binary-contact-entropy]</a>, and adding the initial scale, proves $$m(J)\le1+C\sqrt J+\frac{CJ}{\log J}
       =O(J/\log J).$$ ◻

</details>

The estimate uses the same departure contacts as the original arrangement. The next section bounds the chance of a first contact far above a true cut; that bound will force more contact scales than Proposition <a href="#prop:entropy-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:entropy-upper">14</a> permits.

<a id="sec:posterior"></a>
### Contacts with a path anchored at a random endpoint

The lower contact bound requires a different comparison from the one used in Section <a href="#sec:entropy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:entropy">5</a>. A positive bridge now determines the starting point of the negative path. We control this dependence by retaining the conditional probabilities of the bridge’s possible endpoints. The needed estimate for these probabilities is a finite-vector martingale inequality.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The sum of posterior maxima

The first-moment calculation integrates the nonnegative-martingale maximal inequality, whose classical form appears in (Ville 1939, chap. V, Première section, §2, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Ville1939](#ref-Ville1939)]. The same coordinatewise calculation for posterior vectors appears in Kesselheim, Molinaro, Patton, and Singla (Kesselheim et al. 2026, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[KesselheimMolinaroPattonSingla2026](#ref-KesselheimMolinaroPattonSingla2026)]. To obtain an exponential moment, we also control conditional future increases and the size of each jump. This is the increasing-process energy mechanism of (Kikuchi 1992, Theorem 4 and its corollary) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Kikuchi1992](#ref-Kikuchi1992)]; we give an elementary threshold proof with the required filtration explicit.

<a id="stmt-lem-posterior-maxima"></a>
<div id="lem:posterior-maxima" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:posterior-exponential"></a>
<a id="eq:posterior-event-weight"></a>
**Lemma 17** (Posterior maxima). *Let $m\ge1$, and let $(w_i(e))_{i\ge0}$, $1\le e\le m$, be nonnegative martingales for a filtration $(\mathcal F_i)$, with $\sum_{e=1}^m w_i(e)\le1$ almost surely. Define $$A_i(e)=\max_{0\le j\le i}w_j(e),\qquad
 A_i=\sum_{e=1}^m A_i(e).$$ Write $A_\infty(e)=\lim_{i\to\infty}A_i(e)$ and $A_\infty=\sum_e A_\infty(e)=\lim_{i\to\infty}A_i$. There are absolute constants $c,C>0$ such that $$\label{eq:posterior-exponential}
 \mathbb E\exp\left(\frac{cA_\infty}{\log(m+1)}\right)\le C.$$ Moreover, if $V$ is any event on a joint probability space with this martingale-path marginal and $p=P(V)$, then $$\label{eq:posterior-event-weight}
 \mathbb E[A_\infty\mathbf 1_V]
 \le C\log(m+1)\,p\log(e/p),$$ where the right side is zero when $p=0$. The event $V$ need not belong to any $\mathcal F_i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-posterior-maxima"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-posterior-maxima</summary>

<a id="eq:posterior-future-increase"></a>
*Proof.* Write $B=\log(m+1)$. We first show that, at every almost surely finite stopping time $\rho$, $$\label{eq:posterior-future-increase}
 \mathbb E[A_\infty-A_\rho\mid\mathcal F_\rho]\le B.$$ For a fixed coordinate put $a=A_\rho(e)$ and $p_e=w_\rho(e)$, so that $0\le p_e\le a\le1$. Conditional maximal inequality and integration over levels give, when $a>0$, $$\mathbb E[A_\infty(e)-a\mid\mathcal F_\rho]
 \le\int_a^1\frac{p_e}{u}\,du
 =p_e\log(1/a)\le p_e\log(1/p_e).$$ If $a=0$, the martingale has conditional expectation zero at every later time and contributes zero. The conditional maximal inequality follows first for bounded stopping times and finite horizons; localization and monotone convergence give the displayed version. Summing over $e$ and adding the missing mass $1-\sum_e p_e$ bounds the resulting sum by the entropy of a probability vector on $m+1$ elements, hence by $B$. This proves <a href="#eq:posterior-future-increase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior-future-increase">[eq:posterior-future-increase]</a>.

At each time the increase of a coordinate maximum is at most its current value. Consequently $$A_0\le1,\qquad 0\le A_i-A_{i-1}\le\sum_e w_i(e)\le1.$$ Set $b=2B+1$ and consider the successive thresholds $1+kb$, $k\ge1$. At their first strict crossings, the overshoot is at most one. After a crossing, reaching the next threshold requires a further increase greater than $b-1=2B$. Conditional Markov inequality and <a href="#eq:posterior-future-increase" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior-future-increase">[eq:posterior-future-increase]</a> therefore bound the conditional probability of this next crossing by $1/2$. The first crossing has the same bound since $A_0\le1$. Applying this argument at finite horizons and then letting the horizon increase yields $$P(A_\infty>1+kb)\le2^{-k}.$$ Because $B\ge\log2$, this is an exponential tail on scale $B$, and its integral proves <a href="#eq:posterior-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior-exponential">[eq:posterior-exponential]</a>.

Finally, for $p>0$, conditional Jensen inequality gives $$\exp\left(\frac{c\,\mathbb E[A_\infty\mid V]}{B}\right)
 \le \mathbb E[\exp(cA_\infty/B)\mid V]\le C/p.$$ Multiplication by $p$ and an adjustment of the absolute constant give <a href="#eq:posterior-event-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:posterior-event-weight">[eq:posterior-event-weight]</a>. This last argument uses only a marginal exponential moment, so it applies to events in a larger joint space without any assertion that the martingales remain martingales in an enlarged filtration. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### A first-contact estimate

The comparison of opposing paths through unvisited rows and the use of quenched exit probabilities are related to Zerner’s planar argument (Zerner 2007, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[Zerner2007](#ref-Zerner2007)]. Here the negative path starts at a random endpoint of the positive bridge; the posterior estimate controls the dependence created by that alignment.

We continue to use integer height $x_1$, and write $\pi x\in\mathbb Z^{d-1}$ for the lateral coordinates of $x\in\mathbb Z^d$. Recall that $u_n^+=P_0(T_n<T_{-1})$ and $p_\pm>0$ are the non-backtracking probabilities. The constants below may depend on the environment law and the dimension. We assume only strict positivity of the transition rows; no common ellipticity bound is imposed.

Fix an integer $N\ge3$. Let $Y$ have the annealed bridge law from $0$ to its first arrival at height $N$, conditioned on $T_N<T_{-1}$, and put $E=\pi Y_{T_N}$. Independently sample a negative path $B$ with law $\widehat P_{-e_1}$. Translate it to the random starting site $(N-1,E)$: $$\beta_j=(N-1,E)+B_j,\qquad j\ge0.$$ Thus the relative negative path is independent of the positive bridge; its absolute position depends on the bridge endpoint. A departure of $Y$ means an index $i<T_N$. Every site of the infinite path $\beta$ is a departure site. For a negative path started at height $N-1$, write $D^-$ for the event that it never rises above $N-1$.

<a id="stmt-prop-contact-bound"></a>
<div id="prop:contact-bound" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:first-contact-bound"></a>
**Proposition 18** (First contact after a prescribed height). *In the preceding experiment, let $0<k<r<N$ be integers and define $$\tau=\inf\{i\ge T_k:i<T_N,\ Y_i\in\{\beta_j:j\ge0\}\},
 \qquad
 \Delta=u^+_{r-k}-u^+_{N-k}.$$ An empty infimum is $+\infty$. Then $$\begin{gathered}
\label{eq:first-contact-bound}
 P\left(T_r\le\tau<T_N,\
       \min_{T_k\le i\le\tau}(Y_i)_1\ge k,\
       \|E\|\le N^2\right)\\
 \le C\log(2N)\,\Delta\log^2(e/\Delta),
\end{gathered}$$ with right side zero when $\Delta=0$.*

</div>

The estimate is useful when the remaining gap $N-r$ is small relative to the height $r-k$ already crossed. Lemma <a href="#lem:renewal-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:renewal-tail">12</a> gives $\Delta\le (N-r)F_+(r-k)$; Section <a href="#sec:contradiction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:contradiction">7</a> arranges precisely this separation of scales.

The time $\tau$ refers to the entire negative trajectory and is not asserted to be a stopping time for the positive path. We will enumerate finite prefixes to use its first-contact property. Martingale estimates will instead be stopped at deterministic height barriers.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-contact-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-contact-bound</summary>

<a id="eq:endpoint-cardinality"></a>
*Proof.* Let $\mathcal E_N=\{e\in\mathbb Z^{d-1}:\|e\|\le N^2\}$. Its cardinality satisfies $$\label{eq:endpoint-cardinality}
 \log(|\mathcal E_N|+1)\le C_d\log(2N).$$ We first express the event in <a href="#eq:first-contact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-contact-bound">[eq:first-contact-bound]</a> by raw path weights, then transfer those weights to a shared environment, and only afterward introduce quenched exit probabilities.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### The lower prefix and the endpoint likelihood.

Enumerate a word $a_0$ from $0$ to its first arrival at height $k$, before height $-1$. Its departure sites all have height less than $k$; write $z$ for its terminal site. For each $e\in\mathcal E_N$, enumerate a word $a$ from $z$ to the first contact arrival. The required words stay in heights $k,\ldots,N-1$ and reach height $r$ before or at their terminal time. Let $\operatorname{Dep}(a)$ denote the departure sites of $a$, excluding its terminal site unless that site occurred earlier as a departure.

<a id="eq:prefix-completion-weight"></a>
For any such positive prefix define $$w(a_0a;e)=P_0\bigl(T_N<T_{-1},\ \pi X_{T_N}=e
                   \mid X\text{ begins with }a_0a\bigr).$$ This is a deterministic function of the two words and $e$. The two departure sets of $a_0$ and $a$ are disjoint, so the positive prefix and completion have raw probability $$\label{eq:prefix-completion-weight}
 W(a_0)W(a)w(a_0a;e).$$ For a raw negative walk started at $(N-1,e)$, let $\mathcal B(a)$ be the event that it never rises above $N-1$, avoids $\operatorname{Dep}(a)$ at every time, and visits the terminal site of $a$. The first-contact condition is exactly this avoidance and visit condition. In particular, if the terminal site of $a$ occurred earlier as a departure, the event $\mathcal B(a)$ is empty. Summing <a href="#eq:prefix-completion-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-completion-weight">[eq:prefix-completion-weight]</a> times the raw probability of $\mathcal B(a)$, over these words and endpoints, and dividing by $u_N^+p_-$ gives the probability on the left of <a href="#eq:first-contact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-contact-bound">[eq:first-contact-bound]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### Transfer and unique prefix counting.

<a id="eq:unclassified-lower-transfer"></a>
For a fixed word $a$, the quenched probability of $\mathcal B(a)$ uses no rows at $\operatorname{Dep}(a)$. Indeed the walk can be killed on first arrival at this set, before any row there is used. This remains valid for the infinite avoidance event by passage from finite paths. The departure-row identity of Lemma <a href="#lem:departure-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:departure-transfer">2</a> therefore gives $$\label{eq:unclassified-lower-transfer}
 W(a)P_{(N-1,e)}(\mathcal B(a))
 =\mathbb E\left[p_\omega(a)
       P_{(N-1,e),\omega}(\mathcal B(a))\right],$$ where $p_\omega(a)$ is the quenched product of transition probabilities along the word. This identity has not been restricted by any environment-dependent exit-probability condition.

Keep $W(a_0)$ as a scalar outside the new experiment. In a fresh i.i.d. environment sample a raw walk $Z$ from $z$ and a raw negative walk from $(N-1,e)$, independently conditional on the environment. We call $Z$ the comparison path. Formula <a href="#eq:unclassified-lower-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unclassified-lower-transfer">[eq:unclassified-lower-transfer]</a> expresses the sum over $a$ as an integral over these two full paths. For each realized pair, at most one prefix $a$ of $Z$ satisfies the avoidance and visit conditions: its terminal is the first point of $Z$ in the range of the entire negative path. This proves uniqueness pathwise, without using an optional-stopping assertion at contact. The restrictions that $a$ stays above $k-1$ and reaches $r$ are retained at this stage.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
###### Stopped posteriors on the comparison path.

Fix $a_0$ and consider the original raw positive law conditional on this lower prefix. In its natural continuation filtration, with no environment revealed, the coordinates $$w_i(e)=P_0\bigl(T_N<T_{-1},\ \pi X_{T_N}=e
             \mid a_0,\text{ continuation through time }i\bigr),
 \qquad e\in\mathcal E_N,$$ are nonnegative martingales whose sum is at most one. Stop them on first arrival at height $k-1$ or $N$. All departures up to this stop have height at least $k$. These rows are fresh under conditioning on $a_0$, so the stopped continuation has precisely the same path law as $Z$ stopped at $$\sigma=T_{k-1}(Z)\wedge T_N(Z).$$ Arrival at the lower boundary uses a row at height $k$, not a departure row at $k-1$. Thus the equality includes the terminal arrival, as illustrated in Figure <a href="#fig:fresh-strip" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:fresh-strip">2</a>. The stopping time is almost surely finite by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-supremum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-supremum">3</a>: a path that never leaves this finite height interval would have finite height supremum without tending to negative height infinity. The corresponding quenched assertion holds for almost every environment, simultaneously for all lattice starting sites and integer height intervals.

<a id="eq:comparison-event-weight"></a>
Use these same posterior functions on the observed prefixes of $Z$, and freeze them at $\sigma$. At arrival at $k-1$ their values need not be zero: they still describe success before the original lower barrier $-1$, and that completion may use rows affected by $a_0$. We neither recompute them in the fresh environment nor continue them through a departure below $k$. Put $$M(e)=\sup_{0\le i\le\sigma}w_i(e),\qquad
 S_*=\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}M(e).$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:posterior-maxima" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:posterior-maxima">17</a> and <a href="#eq:endpoint-cardinality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:endpoint-cardinality">[eq:endpoint-cardinality]</a>, applied in the path filtration of the stopped comparison walk, imply uniformly in $a_0$ that $$\label{eq:comparison-event-weight}
 \mathbb E[S_*\mathbf 1_V]
 \le C\log(2N)\,P(V)\log(e/P(V))$$ for every event $V$ on the joint space of the fresh environment and $Z$. In particular, $\mathbb ES_*\le C\log(2N)$. The event-weight inequality, rather than a martingale assertion after revealing the environment, permits the next step.

<figure id="fig:fresh-strip">

<figcaption>Fresh rows for the stopped comparison path. The lower prefix first reaches height $k$ at $z$, and all its departure rows lie below $k$. The continuation, shown with two schematic exit alternatives, stops on arrival at height $k − 1$ or $N$. It has used only rows with heights $k, …, N − 1$, so its stopped path law agrees with the fresh comparison law. The original endpoint posteriors are frozen at this stop, not recomputed under a new completion law. Lateral positions are schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-fresh-strip"></a>

Visual witness description of PDF page 28 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
Coordinate plot with vertical axis labeled "height $x_1$" showing tick marks at $0$, $k-1$, $k$, and $N$. A path drawn with solid black steps labeled "lower prefix $a_0$: departures below $k$" begins at height 0 and reaches point $z$ at height $k$. From point $z$, two continuation alternatives proceed into a shaded region representing heights $k$ through $N-1$: an upper blue stepped path terminating at height $N$ labeled "upper exit: stop at arrival", and a lower dashed purple stepped path terminating at height $k-1$ labeled "lower exit: stop at arrival; no departure from height $k-1$". The stopped path laws agree; the original posteriors are retained."
```


<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Quenched exit-probability bins.

<a id="eq:comparison-bin-probability"></a>
For $k\le y_1<N$ define $$Q_y^\omega=P_{y,\omega}(T_{k-1}<T_N).$$ Almost surely every transition at every site is strictly positive. For each interior site $y$, a finite sequence of downward axial steps reaches $k-1$ before $N$ with positive quenched probability; a finite sequence of upward axial steps reaches $N$ before $k-1$ with positive quenched probability. Thus $0<Q_y^\omega<1$, without a common bound away from either endpoint. Partition these values into dyadic bins $[a,2a)$, where $a=2^{-j}$ and $j\ge1$, and write $\mathcal D_a^\omega=\{y:k\le y_1<N,\ a\le Q_y^\omega<2a\}$ for the sites in one bin. Let $V_a$ be the event that, at or after its first arrival at height $r$ and before $\sigma$, the comparison path visits an interior site with $Q_y^\omega\ge a$. With the environment fixed, stop at the first such visit. The quenched Markov property gives $$\begin{aligned}
 aP(V_a)
 &\le P_z(T_r<T_{k-1}<T_N)\notag\\
 &=u^+_{r-k}-u^+_{N-k}=\Delta.
 \label{eq:comparison-bin-probability}
\end{aligned}$$ The equality follows from nested first-hit events and almost sure exit from the height interval, again by Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-supremum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-supremum">3</a>. These events use the fresh comparison law, not the law conditioned on the lower prefix.

For almost every environment, the raw negative walk has probability at most $2a$ of both satisfying $D^-$ and hitting $\mathcal D_a^\omega$. To see this, take its first visit to that set. On $D^-$ the negative walk has finite first-coordinate supremum, so Lemma <a href="#lem:finite-supremum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:finite-supremum">3</a> implies that its first coordinate tends to $-\infty$. The quenched form of that lemma holds outside a single environment-null set for all lattice starting sites. Consequently, after that first bin visit it must hit $k-1$ before $N$, even if it had previously visited $k-1$. Its conditional probability is at most $Q_y^\omega<2a$ by the quenched Markov property.

<a id="eq:bin-contribution"></a>
We now use the unique prefix established above. In the transferred integral, classify its contact site by its $Q^\omega$ bin and bound its endpoint factor $w(a_0a;e)$ by $M(e)$. Dropping the exact-contact condition leaves $V_a$ for the comparison path and $D^-\cap\{\hbox{the negative path hits }\mathcal D_a^\omega\}$ for the negative path. There is no sum over competing prefixes. After integrating the negative path, every endpoint $e$ leaves the same fresh joint marginal $\mathbb P(d\omega)P_{z,\omega}(dZ)$. Thus the bin’s total contribution, summed over endpoints, is at most $$\label{eq:bin-contribution}
 \begin{split}
 &\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}\mathbb E\left[
     M(e)\mathbf 1_{V_a}
     P_{(N-1,e),\omega}
     (D^-\cap\{\hbox{the path hits }\mathcal D_a^\omega\})\right]\\
 &\hspace{35mm}\le 2a\,\mathbb E\left[\mathbf 1_{V_a}\sum_{e\in\mathcal E_N}M(e)\right]
  =2a\,\mathbb E[S_*\mathbf 1_{V_a}].
 \end{split}$$

If $\Delta=0$, every $V_a$ is null by <a href="#eq:comparison-bin-probability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-bin-probability">[eq:comparison-bin-probability]</a>, proving the required zero bound. Suppose $\Delta>0$. For $a<\Delta$, sum <a href="#eq:bin-contribution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:bin-contribution">[eq:bin-contribution]</a> using $\mathbb ES_*\le C\log(2N)$ and $\sum_{a<\Delta}a\le2\Delta$. For $a\ge\Delta$, use <a href="#eq:comparison-event-weight" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:comparison-event-weight">[eq:comparison-event-weight]</a> and $P(V_a)\le\Delta/a$ to bound the contribution by $$C\log(2N)\,\Delta\log(ea/\Delta).$$ There are $O(\log(e/\Delta))$ such dyadic bins, and their logarithms sum to $O(\log^2(e/\Delta))$. Thus, for each fixed $a_0$, all endpoint and upper-prefix contributions together are at most $$C\log(2N)\,\Delta\log^2(e/\Delta).$$ Finally $\sum_{a_0}W(a_0)=u_k^+\le1$, and $1/(u_N^+p_-)\le1/(p_+p_-)$. Restoring these factors proves <a href="#eq:first-contact-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:first-contact-bound">[eq:first-contact-bound]</a>. The constants lose only these fixed non-backtracking probabilities and the endpoint-cardinality factor. In particular, the argument has not required a lower bound for $Q_y^\omega$ or an inverse-transition moment. ◻

</details>

The estimate controls a contact anywhere after the prescribed height $r$. It therefore applies to a long interval without subdividing the positive path by its successive record heights. In the next section a missing interval of contact depths forces exactly such a late first contact.

<a id="sec:contradiction"></a>
### Many contact scales and the contradiction

We now apply Proposition <a href="#prop:contact-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:contact-bound">18</a> to the opposed arrangement. An interval of depths containing no contact forces the first contact above each positive cut in that interval to occur close to the top. The first-contact estimate makes this unlikely on average over the cuts. We first count scales of *depth*; the finite mean common-block width will then convert this count into the block-index count used in Proposition <a href="#prop:entropy-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:entropy-upper">14</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-contact-lower"></a>
<div id="prop:contact-lower" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *Suppose that both coordinate signs have positive directional-transience probability. In the opposed arrangement of Section <a href="#sec:arrangement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:arrangement">4</a>, let $m(J)$ be the expected number of dyadic common-block index intervals $(2^j,2^{j+1}]$, $1\le j\le J$, containing a departure-site contact, as in <a href="#eq:contact-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:contact-count">[eq:contact-count]</a>. There are constants $c>0$ and $J_0<\infty$ such that $$m(J)\ge \frac{cJ}{\log\log(3+J)}\qquad(J\ge J_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-contact-lower"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-contact-lower</summary>

<a id="eq:lower-parameters"></a>
*Proof.* All constants below may depend on the two slab laws. Choose a large integer $J$ and put $$\label{eq:lower-parameters}
 N=2^{2J},\qquad
 G=\left\lceil4\log_2\log(2+J)\right\rceil+3.$$ For sufficiently large $J$, we have $3\le G<J$. Depth means distance below the initial top of the arrangement. The choice $2^{-G}=O((\log J)^{-4})$ compensates for the logarithmic losses below, while $G=O(\log\log J)$ leaves enough contact scales.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### A bridge ending at a prescribed depth.

Let $\mathcal C_N$ be the event that the positive tape has a cut at depth $N$, and write $$\mathbf Q_N=\mathbf P(\,\cdot\mid\mathcal C_N).$$ The renewal identity gives $\mathbf P(\mathcal C_N)=u_N^+\ge p_+>0$. Under $\mathbf Q_N$, reverse the list of positive slabs through this cut and translate its bottom to the origin. Call the resulting chronological path $Y$, and let $T_a$ denote its first hit of height $a$. Lemma <a href="#lem:exact-bridge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:exact-bridge">11</a> says that $Y$ has law $P_0(\,\cdot\mid T_N<T_{-1})$, stopped at $T_N$. The steps within each slab retain their original order. Denote the lateral endpoint by $E$, so that the endpoint is $(N,E)$. The negative trajectory starts at $(N-1,E)$ and, relative to that starting point, has the independent law $\widehat P_{-e_1}$.

<a id="eq:lower-endpoint-tail"></a>
The endpoint has a useful elementary tail bound. On $\mathcal C_N$ there are at most $N$ positive slabs before depth $N$, since their widths are positive integers. The norm of their total lateral displacement is at most the sum of their radii, hence at most the sum of the first $N$ positive tape radii. Proposition <a href="#prop:radius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:radius">8</a> and Markov’s inequality give $$\label{eq:lower-endpoint-tail}
 \mathbb E_{\mathbf Q_N}|E|
 \le \frac{N E_+R}{p_+},
 \qquad
 \mathbf Q_N\{|E|>N^2\}\le \frac{C}{N}.$$ This uses a spatial radius moment only.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### An uncovered scale forces a late first contact.

Call $l\ge0$ a contact depth label if a contact occurs at some integer depth in $[2^l,2^{l+1})$. For $G<l\le J$, let $U_l$ be the event that none of the labels $l-G,l-G+1,\ldots,l$ is a contact depth label. Equivalently, there is no contact at depths in [(eq:uncovered-first-contact)](#eq:uncovered-first-contact)$[2^{l-G},2^{l+1})$.

Fix a positive cut depth $$s\in I_l:=\bigl[2^l,\tfrac32\,2^l\bigr]\cap\mathbb Z,
 \qquad k=N-s.$$ After the list reversal, this cut occurs at the bridge’s first hit $T_k$, and the remaining bridge stays at or above height $k$. Indeed, every earlier slab has departure heights below $k$, while every later slab has departure heights at least $k$. This is a property of the words, without a stopping-time assertion about the cut.

<a id="eq:uncovered-first-contact"></a>
There is a contact after $T_k$ and before $T_N$: the last positive departure is $(N-1,E)$, the starting departure site of the negative trajectory. Let $\tau$ be the first contact since $T_k$, defined pathwise against the entire negative trajectory. Its depth is at most $s<2^{l+1}$. On $U_l$, it must therefore be strictly less than $2^{l-G}$. With $$r=N-2^{l-G},$$ the nearest-neighbor path must have reached height $r$ before this contact. Thus $$\label{eq:uncovered-first-contact}
 T_r\le\tau<T_N,
 \qquad Y_n\cdot e_1\ge k\quad(T_k\le n\le\tau).$$ No restriction on drawdowns from the running maximum is needed.

<a id="eq:lower-delta"></a>
<a id="eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound"></a>
For each *deterministic* $s\in I_l$, let $\mathcal A_{l,s}$ be the event in <a href="#eq:uncovered-first-contact" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:uncovered-first-contact">[eq:uncovered-first-contact]</a>, together with $|E|\le N^2$, whether or not $s$ is a cut. Proposition  <a href="#prop:contact-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:contact-bound">18</a>, with upper stopping height $N$, applies to this event. Its width-probability difference satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \Delta_{l,s}
 &=u^+_{s-2^{l-G}}-u^+_s\notag\\
 &\le 2^{l-G}F_+(s-2^{l-G})
 \le 2^{-G}a_l,
 \qquad a_l:=2^l F_+(2^{l-1}),
 \label{eq:lower-delta}
\end{aligned}$$ because $s-2^{l-G}\ge2^{l-1}$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:renewal-tail" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:renewal-tail">12</a> applies. The same Lemma gives <a id="eq:lower-summable-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:lower-summable-tail}
 A:=\sum_{l\ge1}a_l<\infty.$$ Writing $\phi(x)=x\log^2(e/x)$ for $0<x\le1$ and $\phi(0)=0$, the first-contact estimate is $$\label{eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound}
 \mathbf Q_N(\mathcal A_{l,s})
 \le C\log(2N)\,\phi(\Delta_{l,s}).$$

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### Averaging over the cuts.

<a id="eq:cut-density-conditioning"></a>
Let $\mu_+=E_+L$. The renewal-count strong law yields, almost surely under the positive tape law, $$\#\{\hbox{positive cuts in }I_l\}
 =\frac{2^{l-1}}{\mu_+}+o(2^l)
 \qquad(l\longrightarrow\infty).$$ Fix $c_0=1/(4\mu_+)$, and let $\mathcal B_J$ be the event that each interval $I_l$, $G<l\le J$, contains at least $c_0 2^l$ positive cuts. Since $G=G(J)\to\infty$, the eventual almost-sure estimate implies $\mathbf P(\mathcal B_J^c)=o(1)$. Moreover, $$\label{eq:cut-density-conditioning}
 \mathbf Q_N(\mathcal B_J^c)
 \le \frac{\mathbf P(\mathcal B_J^c)}{p_+}=o(1).$$ In particular, no independence between the cut at $N$ and the density event is required.

<a id="eq:cut-averaging"></a>
On $\mathcal B_J\cap\{|E|\le N^2\}$, every uncovered label $l$ produces $\mathcal A_{l,s}$ for each actual cut $s\in I_l$. Consequently, $$\label{eq:cut-averaging}
 \mathbf 1_{U_l}\mathbf 1_{\mathcal B_J}\mathbf 1_{\{|E|\le N^2\}}
 \le \frac1{c_0 2^l}\sum_{s\in I_l}\mathbf 1_{\mathcal A_{l,s}}.$$ The right side has dropped the cut requirement on $s$. This permits the deterministic-barrier estimate <a href="#eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound">[eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound]</a>; the number of summands is $O(2^l)$ and cancels the averaging factor.

<a id="eq:weighted-tail-log"></a>
We next check that the logarithms in this estimate do not require a quantitative tail rate. For $J\ge3$, put $$B_J=\sum_{l=1}^J a_l\log_+^2(1/a_l),
 \qquad \log_+x:=\max\{\log x,0\},$$ where a zero summand is interpreted as zero. Terms with $a_l\ge J^{-2}$ contribute at most $4A\log^2 J$. On $(0,J^{-2})$, the function $x\log^2(1/x)$ is increasing, so the remaining at most $J$ terms contribute at most $4J^{-1}\log^2 J$. Therefore $$\label{eq:weighted-tail-log}
 B_J\le 4(A+J^{-1})\log^2 J.$$

<a id="eq:few-uncovered"></a>
For sufficiently large $J$, $2^{-G}a_l\le e^{-1}$ uniformly in $l$, since $a_l\le A$. The function $\phi$ is increasing on $[0,e^{-1}]$. Thus <a href="#eq:lower-delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-delta">[eq:lower-delta]</a> gives, uniformly over $s\in I_l$, $$\phi(\Delta_{l,s})\le C2^{-G}a_l
 \bigl(G+1+\log_+(1/a_l)\bigr)^2.$$ Taking expectations in <a href="#eq:cut-averaging" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-averaging">[eq:cut-averaging]</a>, then using <a href="#eq:lower-endpoint-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-endpoint-tail">[eq:lower-endpoint-tail]</a>, <a href="#eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound">[eq:lower-fixed-cut-bound]</a>, and <a href="#eq:cut-density-conditioning" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-density-conditioning">[eq:cut-density-conditioning]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{l=G+1}^J\mathbf Q_N(U_l)
 &\le J\mathbf Q_N(\mathcal B_J^c)+\frac{CJ}{N}
   +C\log(2N)\,2^{-G}\bigl[A(G+1)^2+B_J\bigr]\notag\\
 &=o(J).
 \label{eq:few-uncovered}
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed, $\log(2N)=O(J)$, while the choice  <a href="#eq:lower-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lower-parameters">[eq:lower-parameters]</a> gives $2^{-G}=O(\log^{-4}(2+J))$. By <a href="#eq:weighted-tail-log" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-tail-log">[eq:weighted-tail-log]</a>, the last term is $O(J/\log^2(2+J))$.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Removing the conditioning.

<a id="eq:height-count-conditioned"></a>
<a id="eq:height-count-unconditioned"></a>
Let $H_J$ count contact depth labels in $\{1,\ldots,J\}$. Equation <a href="#eq:few-uncovered" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:few-uncovered">[eq:few-uncovered]</a> and Markov’s inequality imply that with $\mathbf Q_N$-probability tending to one, at most $J/4$ of the tested labels are uncovered. Since $G=o(J)$, at least $J/2$ are then covered. A single contact depth label $h$ can cover only the labels $h,h+1,\ldots,h+G$. Hence $$\label{eq:height-count-conditioned}
 \mathbf Q_N\left\{H_J\ge\frac{J}{2(G+1)}\right\}
 \longrightarrow1.$$ For large $J$, all depths counted here are less than $2^{J+1}<N$. On $\mathcal C_N$ they are therefore fully represented in the positive list ending at depth $N$. Undoing the translation and list reversal leaves exactly the contacts in the original opposed arrangement. Thus <a href="#eq:height-count-conditioned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-count-conditioned">[eq:height-count-conditioned]</a> implies $$\label{eq:height-count-unconditioned}
 \mathbf P\left\{H_J\ge\frac{J}{2(G+1)}\right\}
 \ge p_+-o(1).$$ Only a probability bounded away from zero is asserted after removing the conditioning. This is enough for an expectation lower bound.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### From depths to common-block indices.

<a id="eq:depth-index-comparison"></a>
Write $K_1,K_2,\ldots$ for the common-block widths and $W_i=K_1+\cdots+K_i$, with $W_0=0$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:common-blocks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:common-blocks">13</a>, $\mu:=\mathbf E K_1<\infty$ and $W_i/i\to\mu$ almost surely. In particular, eventually $$\frac\mu2 i\le W_i\le2\mu i.$$ An integer depth $z\ge1$ belongs to the unique block $$i(z)=\min\{i\ge1:W_i\ge z\},
 \qquad W_{i(z)-1}<z\le W_{i(z)}.$$ The departure-height convention makes this true even at a common cut depth. For every sufficiently large $z$, the strong-law bounds give $$\label{eq:depth-index-comparison}
 \frac{z}{2\mu}\le i(z)<\frac{2z}{\mu}+1.$$ The finite initial blocks, however large their widths, disappear once $z$ exceeds their cumulative width.

Define the dyadic index label of a block $i>1$ by $j(i)=\lceil\log_2 i\rceil-1$, so that $2^{j(i)}<i\le2^{j(i)+1}$. It follows from <a href="#eq:depth-index-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:depth-index-comparison">[eq:depth-index-comparison]</a> that there is a deterministic integer $c_1$, depending only on $\mu$, such that eventually <a id="eq:bounded-label-shift"></a>$$\label{eq:bounded-label-shift}
 |j(i(z))-l|\le c_1
 \quad\hbox{whenever }2^l\le z<2^{l+1}.$$ Almost surely this holds above a finite random depth. Therefore the probability that it holds for every contact with $\lceil\sqrt J\rceil\le l\le J$ tends to one.

Intersect this event with the event in <a href="#eq:height-count-unconditioned" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:height-count-unconditioned">[eq:height-count-unconditioned]</a>. The intersection still has probability at least $p_+-o(1)$. Discarding the first $\lceil\sqrt J\rceil$ depth labels loses $o(J/(G+1))$ labels. Each remaining contact depth label supplies a contact block whose index label lies within $c_1$ of it. Conversely, one index label can account for at most $2c_1+1$ depth labels. Thus, on the intersection, at least $c_2J/(G+1)$ of the index labels $1,\ldots,J+c_1$ contain a contact, for a fixed $c_2>0$ and large $J$. Taking expectations yields $$m(J+c_1)\ge\frac{c_3J}{G+1}.$$ Since $G+1=O(\log\log(3+J))$, replacing $J+c_1$ by the argument of $m$ proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-contact-lower-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-contact-lower</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Fix any nonzero real direction $\ell$. Suppose that $0<P_0(A_\ell)<1$. Lemma <a href="#lem:sign-union" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sign-union">7</a> implies that $P_0(A_{-\ell})>0$. The spatial-radius estimate and Proposition <a href="#prop:coordinate-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:coordinate-reduction">10</a> then supply a coordinate direction in which both signs have positive probability. Reflecting and relabeling that coordinate gives the setting of Propositions <a href="#prop:entropy-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:entropy-upper">14</a> and <a href="#prop:contact-lower" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:contact-lower">19</a>. They imply simultaneously $$m(J)=O\!\left(\frac{J}{\log J}\right)
 \quad\hbox{and}\quad
 m(J)\ge\frac{cJ}{\log\log(3+J)}$$ for all sufficiently large $J$, which is impossible. Thus $P_0(A_\ell)\in\{0,1\}$.

The reduction applies to every fixed real $\ell$ without rational approximation. Strict ellipticity supplied the fresh-row probabilities; the spatial-radius moment was derived under coexistence. Neither a moment of a slab’s duration nor a speed assumption entered the argument. ◻

</details>

Word interiors and lateral positions are not depicted; the arrows do not assert monotonicity of the words.",

      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  plus/.style={draw=blue!65!black,line width=1pt},\n  minus/.style={draw=red!65!black,line width=1pt},\n  guide/.style={draw=black!35,densely dashed,line width=.4pt}\n]\n  \\node[anchor=west,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (0,.85)\n    {(a) Common departure bands};\n  \\node[align=center] at (2.0,.35) {positive\\\\tape};\n  \\node[align=center] at (3.9,.35) {negative\\\\tape};\n  \\fill[black!4] (.9,-1.3) rectangle (5.1,-2.6);\n  \\foreach \\yy/\\lab in {0/0,-1.3/W_1,-2.6/W_2,-3.9/W_3} {\n    \\draw[guide] (.9,\\yy)--(5.1,\\yy);\n    \\node[anchor=east] at (.75,\\yy) {$\\lab$};\n  }\n  \\foreach \\top/\\bottom/\\j in {0/-1.3/1,-1.3/-2.6/2,-2.6/-3.9/3} {\n    \\draw[plus,->] (2.0,\\bottom+.18)--(2.0,\\top-.18);\n    \\draw[minus,->] (3.9,\\top-.18)--(3.9,\\bottom+.18);\n    \\node at (5.45,{(\\top+\\bottom)/2}) {$K_{\\j}$};\n  }\n  \\draw[->,black!70] (-.15,-.30)--(-.15,-3.60);\n  \\node[rotate=90,anchor=south] at (-.45,-1.95) {progress $-x_1$};\n  \\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.95,-4.35)\n    {Arrows indicate orientation, not paths.\\\\Both tapes are placed downwards.};\n\n  \\begin{scope}[xshift=7.0cm]\n    \\node[anchor=west,font=\\small\\bfseries] at (-.15,.85)\n      {(b) The one-level offset};\n    \\node at (1.4,.30) {$+$};\n    \\node at (3.2,.30) {$-$};\n    \\fill[black!6] (.7,0) rectangle (3.85,-2.7);\n    \\foreach \\yy/\\lab in {0/s,-.8/{s+1},-2.7/{s+L},-3.5/{s+L+1}} {\n      \\draw[guide] (.7,\\yy)--(3.85,\\yy);\n      \\node[anchor=east] at (.55,\\yy) {$\\lab$};\n    }\n    \\draw[plus,->] (1.4,-2.7)--(1.4,0);\n    \\draw[minus,->] (3.2,-.8)--(3.2,-3.5);\n    \\fill[blue!65!black] (1.4,-2.7) circle (2.1pt);\n    \\draw[plus,fill=white] (1.4,0) circle (2.1pt);\n    \\fill[red!65!black] (3.2,-.8) circle (2.1pt);\n    \\draw[minus,fill=white] (3.2,-3.5) circle (2.1pt);\n    \\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.28,-1.72)\n      {departure\\\\heights};\n    \\node[align=center,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.1,-4.10)\n      {Both words depart only at integer progress\\\\in $(s,s+L]$; terminal arrivals are excluded.};\n  \\end{scope}\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram consisting of two panels. Panel (a) titled \"(a) Common departure bands\" depicts two vertical columns labeled \"positive tape\" and \"negative tape\" with a top boundary at 0. A vertical axis along the left is labeled \"progress $-x_1$\" directed downwards. Three shaded horizontal bands spanning both tapes are labeled $W_1, W_2, W_3$ on the left and $K_1, K_2, K_3$ on the right. Upward blue arrows on the positive tape and downward red arrows on the negative tape indicate chronological orientation. Labels below state: \"Arrows indicate orientation, not paths.\" and \"Both tapes are placed downwards.\" Panel (b) titled \"(b) The one-level offset\" shows two columns labeled \"$+$\" and \"$-$\" with vertical progress tick marks at $s$, $s+1$, $s+L$, and $s+L+1$. A shaded vertical bar between $s+1$ and $s+L$ is labeled \"departure heights\". An upward blue arrow runs between $s+L$ and $s+1$ in the \"$+$\" column, and a downward red arrow runs between $s+1$ and $s+L$ in the \"$-$\" column, connecting via a dashed segment to an open circle arrival at $s+L+1$. Below panel (b), text states: \"Both words depart only at integer progress in $(s, s + L]$; terminal arrivals are excluded.\""
    },
    {
      "caption": "Fresh rows for the stopped comparison path. The lower prefix first reaches height $k$ at $z$, and all its departure rows lie below $k$. The continuation, shown with two schematic exit alternatives, stops on arrival at height $k − 1$ or $N$. It has used only rows with heights $k, …, N − 1$, so its stopped path law agrees with the fresh comparison law. The original endpoint posteriors are frozen at this stop, not recomputed under a new completion law. Lateral positions are schematic.",
      "figure_id": "fig:fresh-strip",
      "pdf_page": 28,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:fresh-strip",
          "exact_quote": "Fresh rows for the stopped comparison path. The lower prefix\n first reaches height $k$ at $z$, and all its departure rows lie below\n $k$. The continuation, shown with two schematic exit alternatives,\n stops on arrival at height $k-1$ or $N$",
          "source_object": "fig:0002"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n  x=1cm,y=1cm,>=Stealth,\n  every node/.style={font=\\small},\n  guide/.style={draw=black!35,densely dashed,line width=.4pt},\n  pathline/.style={draw=blue!65!black,line width=1pt}\n]\n  \\fill[black!5] (0,0) rectangle (11.4,1.1);\n  \\fill[blue!4] (0,1.1) rectangle (11.4,4.6);\n  \\foreach \\yy/\\lab in {0/{0},.7/{k-1},1.1/{k},4.6/{N}} {\n    \\draw[guide] (0,\\yy)--(11.4,\\yy);\n    \\node[anchor=east] at (-.15,\\yy) {$\\lab$};\n  }\n  \\draw[->,black!70] (-.8,.15)--(-.8,4.45);\n  \\node[rotate=90,anchor=south] at (-1.0,2.3) {height $x_1$};\n\n  \\draw[black!65,line width=1pt,->]\n    (.65,0)--(.65,.32)--(1.05,.32)--(1.05,.58)\n    --(1.45,.58)--(1.45,.88)--(1.75,.88)--(1.75,1.1);\n  \\fill (1.75,1.1) circle (2pt);\n  \\node[anchor=north west] at (1.85,1.04) {$z$};\n  \\node[anchor=west,font=\\footnotesize] at (2.65,-.35)\n    {lower prefix $a_0$: departures below $k$};\n\n  \\draw[pathline,->]\n    (1.75,1.1)--(1.75,1.55)--(2.2,1.55)--(2.2,2.1)\n    --(2.65,2.1)--(2.65,1.85)--(3.2,1.85)--(3.2,2.55)\n    --(3.65,2.55)--(3.65,3.25)--(4.05,3.25)--(4.05,3.9)\n    --(4.45,3.9)--(4.45,4.6);\n  \\draw[pathline,fill=white] (4.45,4.6) circle (2.4pt);\n  \\node[anchor=south] at (4.45,4.77) {upper exit: stop at arrival};\n\n  \\draw[pathline,densely dashed,->]\n    (3.2,2.55)--(4.6,2.55)--(4.6,2.15)--(5.0,2.15)\n    --(5.0,1.75)--(5.45,1.75)--(5.45,1.1)--(5.9,1.1)\n    --(5.9,.7);\n  \\draw[pathline,fill=white] (5.9,.7) circle (2.4pt);\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,font=\\footnotesize,fill=white,inner sep=2pt] at (6.35,.76)\n    {lower exit: stop at arrival;\\\\no departure from height $k-1$};\n\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left] at (6.3,3.6)\n    {Rows at heights $k,\\ldots,N-1$\\\\are fresh relative to $a_0$.};\n  \\node[anchor=west,align=left,font=\\footnotesize] at (6.3,2.25)\n    {The two exit alternatives are schematic.\\\\The stopped path laws agree;\\\\the original posteriors are retained.};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Coordinate plot with vertical axis labeled \"height $x_1$\" showing tick marks at $0$, $k-1$, $k$, and $N$. A path drawn with solid black steps labeled \"lower prefix $a_0$: departures below $k$\" begins at height 0 and reaches point $z$ at height $k$. From point $z$, two continuation alternatives proceed into a shaded region representing heights $k$ through $N-1$: an upper blue stepped path terminating at height $N$ labeled \"upper exit: stop at arrival\", and a lower dashed purple stepped path terminating at height $k-1$ labeled \"lower exit: stop at arrival; no departure from height $k-1$\". Side annotations state: \"Rows at heights $k, \\dots, N - 1$ are fresh relative to $a_0$.\" and \"The two exit alternatives are schematic. The stopped path laws agree; the original posteriors are retained.\""
    }
